2. RECOGNITION 
  A.    2015 – 2016 Senior Scholar Athletes  
It is a pleasure this evening to welcome and acknowledge our senior scholar 
athletes from winter and spring sports.  To achieve this distinction, a student 
must maintain a 3.5 cumulative grade point average while participating in an 
athletic program.   
As your name is called, please come forward.

2.       RECOGNITION 
C. Principal Appreciation Month 
Throughout the State of Michigan, February has traditionally been celebrated as 
Principal Appreciation Month, in recognition of the outstanding work of our 
building-level educational leaders. In Troy, we are fortunate to have an 
exemplary group of principals dedicated to the success of every student in the 
District. This evening, we will pass a resolution to commend our elementary and 
secondary principals for their exceptional commitment to exc ellence in the Troy 
School District.  
WHEREAS, energetic and inspiring school leadership is essential if teachers, 
schools and districts are to ensure that all Troy students are college  and career 
ready; and, 
WHEREAS, K-12 school principals play a vital  role in the success of students 
and act as the liaison between the school and the community it serves, ensuring 
that parents and the community are aware of student and school achievements; 
and, 
WHEREAS, Troy School District principals are dedicated,  quality educational 
professionals committed to providing sound leadership and guidance to their 
staff and students; and,  
WHEREAS, during this month, we join with educators, parents and students 
throughout Michigan to raise awareness of the importance of ed ucational 
leadership, and to recognize and thank all principals working in Troy for their 
exemplary service; 
NOW THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED , that the Troy Board of Education 
officially recognizes February as Principal Appreciation Month in Troy and thanks 
our dedicated building leaders for all they do for our students and staff.    
RECOMMENDED ACTION:  Read and adopt resolution. 
  Moved by:     
  Supported by:

     2. D.   02.16.16 
2.   RECOGNITION  
D.   TSD Mission Moment—Barnard Fourth Graders – Oakland County 
Clean Water Calendar Contest 
Students at Barnard Elementary show their passion for protecting local fresh 
water sources through their participation in the annual Kids’ Clean Water 
Calendar Contest sponsored by the Oakland Co unty Water Resources 
Commission. This is part of the first unit in Michigan Studies that focuses on 
the Great Lakes ecosystem. Each year for the past eight years, Barnard fourth 
graders have had their artwork chosen from hundreds of entries throughout 
the county to be featured in the annual calendars. After a lesson in Michigan 
Studies class on the im portance of protecting the Great Lakes Watershed, all 
fourth grade Barnard students again submitted a drawing for the contest this 
year. With over 800 entries in the county, Barnard is so proud to have FOUR 
grand winners with five other students’ drawings featured in the 2016 
calendar.

WHEREAS, there may be in and may, hereafter from time

Troy  School  District,  certain  public  monies  belonging  to 
or  held  for  the  State,  County,  or  other  political  units  of 
the State or otherwise held according to law, and 
WHEREAS,  under  the  laws  of  Michigan,  this  Board  is 
required  to  provide  by  Resolution  for  the  deposit  of  all 
public  monies,  including  tax  monies  coming  into  the 
hands of said Treasurer, on one or more banks, hereafter 
called bank(s) to be designated in such Resolution.

RECOGNITION  
celebrates  the  work  of  a  very  important  group  of 
individuals:  The  Board  of  Education.  Troy  is  truly 
tireless,  professional,  and  passionate  about  improving 
the opportunities for our students and our families. This 
evening, Board members were presented with a gift that 
represents  our  students  and  staff,  the  very  people  they 
work so hard to serve.  The gift is a token of appreciation 
that  will  allow  Board  members  to  enjoy  some  of  the 
talents that our students bring to the Troy School District, 
under  the  direction  of  some  very  dedicated  fine  arts 
teachers.  Each  Board  member  was  presented  with  a 
professional  photograph  of  the  mosaic  art  that  was 
created for the new DMC‐Children’s Hospital in Troy. The 
photos were printed, matted and prepared by Athens art 
teacher  Luba  Sordyl  and  represent  student  work  from 
across  the  district.    The  District  administration  hopes 
that it will serve as a reminder of the sincere appreciation 
and the support of our Fine Arts programs. Our Board is 
truly a group that has given so much to our students and 
the  District  is  grateful  to  have  each  and  every  one  of 
them. 
TSD Mission Moment – Troy 
High Students’ “Caring for 
special  group  of  Troy  High  students  and  staff  who  came 
life‐threatening disease. The District is very proud of the 
school wide effort teacher Harriet Clark and her Personal 
Communication classes along with the Troy High Student 
Government  put  towards  this  initiative.   Their  efforts 
raised over $31,500 for the Karen Barrientos Cancer fund. 
Karen  is  an  11th  grade  student  at  Troy  High  with  Non‐
Hodgkins  Lymphoma.    Dr.  Machesky  invited  Mr.  Remo 
Roncone, principal of Troy High, to come up and share a 
little bit more about the students’ efforts. A few students 
and staff members who organized the projects were then 
asked  to  come  up  to  say  a  few  words  then  receive 
recognition from the Board.  
President  Philippart  called  for  a  brief  recess  to  provide 
an opportunity for members of the audience to depart.

Maxwell  Fanroy,  student  representative  from  Athens 
High  School,  reported  that  it  is  finals  week  and  that 
will  be  supporting  the  Joan  Rose  Foundation,  which  is 
their  first  non‐local  charity.   They  will  be  helping 
children in Haiti through a system similar to Boys & Girls 
Club  by  providing  food,  classes  and  activities.      He 
mentioned the various events planned for the week and 
shared  that  a  pep  assembly  and  Charity  Dance  will  be 
held at the end of that week to celebrate.  
Snigdha Suvarna, student representative from Troy High 
School,  reported  that  students  are  studying  through 
finals  this  week  and  that  the  Newspaper  staff  is  selling 
Midterm  Survival  Kits,  which  consist  of  juice,  mints, 
chocolate,  and  a  stress  ball.   She  shared  that  their 
BuildOn organization is raising money to help a school in 
Haiti.   She  announced  that  the  regional  HOSA 
competition is scheduled for this weekend and that over 
150 Troy High students will be participating.  She invited 
everyone  to  attend  the  Troy  High  Orchestra  Gala 
Concert on January 30th at 7 p.m.   She also shared that 
five  juniors  were  selected  into  the  final  round  of  the 
Verizon  Innovation  App  Challenge  and  asked  people  to 
go online and vote for the THS group. 
Matt  Moroski,  student  representative  from  Niles 
Community  High  School,  reported  that  over  50%  of 
of  the  entire  Niles  student  population  is  currently 
enrolled  in  college  classes.   He  shared  that  Student 
Government  delivered  60  coats  to  Grace  Centers  of 
Hope  and  they  are  currently  working  to  develop  after‐
school tutoring.   He also shared that Forensics students 
toured  the  Michigan  State  Police  Crime  Lab  and 
participated in a fictional crime scene investigation.   He 
announced that Drama Club students are working on a 
spring play, The Brothers Grim Spectacularthon.    
Victoria  Malkoun,  student  representative  from 
International  Academy  East,  talked  about  the 
sophomores’  Personal  Project  Exhibition,  which  took

M‐Step Results & County 
Learning  reviewed  assessment  results  for  English 
Language Arts (ELA) and Mathematics in grades 3, 8 and 
11, Science in grades 4, 7, 11, and Social Studies in grades 
5, 8, and 11.   He reviewed test standards and cut scores 
overall  the  District  had  positive  results  compared  to  the 
region and that the results reflect the work of continuous 
improvement  in  all  schools.    He  explained  that  Science 
results were lower than the region with exception of 11th 
grade.   He  also  discussed  utilizing  computers  for  test 
administration  and  the  preparation  for  test  modality  on 
future assessments.  
There  was  some  discussion  among  Board  members, 
Superintendent  Machesky  and  Mr.  Dziatczak  regarding 
the  work  being  done  in  the  Teaching  and  Learning 
Department and the high quality of instruction across the 
District.

From: Machesky, Richard
To: Gary Hauff; Holmes, Judy; Ida Edmunds; Schmidt, Karl; Nancy Philippart ; Paula Fleming; Miletti, Todd;
Underwood, Wendy; Wendy Underwood2; Wendy Underwood3
Cc: Cabinet
Subject: FW: Weiming Update
Date: Friday, January 22, 2016 9:10:36 AM
Good morning Board,
See the message below from Mark regarding our ability to partner with Oakland Schools for the
purposes of utilizing their SEVIS certification for F1 Visas. As a result, we will not be entering into a
MOU with Weiming and will need to start the process of attaining our own SEVIS certification should
we wish to host students that hold an F1 Visa. The process of SEVIS certification is arduous and
would take more than 9 months to complete. Cabinet will discuss the possibility of pursuing the
SEVIS certification but at this time I don’t feel comfortable allocating staff resources towards this
endeavor. I will share more information as we have a chance to discuss this matter further.  
Dear Rich,
Well, I tried my hardest, but I could not make the potential partnership with Weiming work.  Oakland
Schools will not support our work with Weiming.  When undergoing the SEVIS certification work a few
years back, Oakland Schools established and executed a very specific process of selecting F-1 visa
exchange organizations with which to do business.  Weiming was not one of those for several reasons,
foremost because they never submitted RFP documents to be considered for Oakland Schools endorsed
diploma-granting exchanges.  Consequently, Weiming only does business with schools/districts having
achieved SEVIS certification on their own, as Oakland Schools does not and has never authorized
exchanges with that company.
If we want to partner with Weiming in the future, we would need to obtain SEVIS certification on our own
we would need to partner with Educatius, the organization with which we did not enjoy doing business
over the previous two school years.  Districts hosting Educatius students receive the per pupil allocation
plus an additional $2000.  
I will be contacting Kendra Hillary at Weiming tomorrow to inform her of our inability to partner with their
firm.  Furthermore, I will let Remo know that we will not be able to make this happen.  Let’s talk soon
about future possibilities.  Thanks for your patience as I investigated the possibility of this new and unique
partnership.
- Mark
Warm regards,
Rich
Rich Machesky Ed.D.
Superintendent

(see https://www.ice.gov/sevis/schools
).  Or, if we still want to host F-1 students through Oakland Schools,

8. BUSINESS 
A  CONSIDERATION:  Roofing Program

February 8, 2016 
Via Email Only  Troy School District 4400 Livernois Road Troy, Michigan 48098  Attn: Mr. Rick West  Asst. Superintendent, Business Services 
Direct Dial No.: (248) 988-5695 Email: KSutton@LuskAlbertson.com 
Re: Lusk Albertson Policy Service - Updated Dear Mr. West:  We are pleased the Troy School District is considering retaining Lusk Albertson School Policy Services (LA) to help prepare its Board bylaws, policies, and administrative regulations.  It was my pleasure to meet with the Board of Education on February 2, 2016 to discuss our service.  If the District wishes to move forward, to confirm the terms of LA’s engagement for this project, please counter-sign where indicated below and forward a copy to my attention.  LA Bylaws, Policies, and Administrative Regulations.  LA will provide the District with a standard set of bylaws, policies, and administrative regulations.  As discussed, LA’s standard bylaws, policies, and administrative regulations are much more concise than the District’s current version.  Among other things, LA’s documents do not attempt to re-state the law, as the District is obligated to follow the law regardless of its policies and administrative regulations.  We can deliver the standard bylaws and policies upon engagement.  We are still in the process of completing our standard administrative regulations, which we anticipate will be finished by the end of this month.  LA will also obtain and incorporate input from the Board of Education and Administration as necessary to finalize the District’s documents.  Copyrighting and Branding.  The District understands and acknowledges the efforts undertaken by LA to create model bylaws, policies, and administrative regulations, as well as LA’s intellectual property rights in those products.  In recognition of this fact, the District agrees that all bylaws, policies and administrative regulations prepared by LA will be copyrighted and will be and remain the property of LA, even if such model policies are modified consistent with District input. The District understands that it will have LA’s license to use them.  Notwithstanding the foregoing, it is expressly understood and agreed that the District will continue to have license to use bylaws, policies, and administrative regulations created as a result 
128
	
of this agreement even if the District decides, at some point in the future, to discontinue its relationship with LA’s School Policy Service. The District agrees that it will not facilitate the unauthorized use of LA’s bylaws, policies, or administrative regulations.  To this end, the District understands and agrees the final bylaws, policies, and administrative regulations will include a small and tasteful, but legible, symbol reflecting LA’s ownership rights.  LA agrees that it will consult with the District regarding the manner in which the symbol will be presented to the public.  Cost.  The cost of the initial set of bylaws and policies is $5,000.00, payable upon delivery.  The cost of the initial set of administrative regulations is $10,000.00, payable upon delivery.  These flat fees include whatever time it takes LA attorneys to obtain the necessary input from the Board of Education and Administration to create the final complete sets of bylaws, policies and administrative regulations.  The cost of updates to bylaws and policies is $1,500.00 annually.  The cost of updates to administrative regulations is $2,500.00 annually.  The District will be billed for annual updates on or about December 1st of every year.  The District will receive advance notice of any price increases.  Opt-Out.  The District may elect to opt-out of the LA Policy Service by providing LA with notice, in writing, at least 30 days before December 1st of any year.    Again, thank you for considering LA’s Policy Service for the District’s bylaws, policies, and administrative regulations.  We look forward to the opportunity to work with you on these projects. Very truly yours, LUSK ALBERTSON, PLC 
 Kevin T. Sutton Countersigned:  TROY SCHOOL DISTRICT  By:    Its:    Dated:
